  "old_string": "\\section{Inter-Annotator Agreement on AdvBench}\n\\label{app:iaa}\n\nTo assess annotation reliability, two LLM annotators (Qwen3-32B and GPT-4o-mini) independently labeled 388 AdvBench B-class traces for jailbreak status and CP position (\\cref{tab:iaa}).\n\n\\begin{table}[h]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{Inter-annotator agreement on AdvBench B-class traces (388 total). CP position metrics computed on 51 traces where both annotators identified a jailbreak.}\n\\label{tab:iaa}\n\\begin{tabular}{lc}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Metric} & \\textbf{Value} \\\\\n\\midrule\n\\textit{Jailbreak classification} \\\\\n\\quad Cohen's $\\kappa$ & 0.606 (moderate) \\\\\n\\quad Raw agreement & 87.4\\% \\\\\n\\quad GPT-4o-mini JB count & 99 / 388 \\\\\n\\quad Qwen3-32B JB count & 52 / 388 \\\\\n\\midrule\n\\textit{CP position (51 shared JB)} \\\\\n\\quad Exact match & 37\\% \\\\\n\\quad Within 5 steps & 47\\% \\\\\n\\quad Mean abs.\\ difference & 8.5 steps \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\nLLM annotators achieve only moderate agreement on jailbreak classification ($\\kappa{=}0.606$) and low CP position consistency (37\\% exact match). GPT-4o-mini is substantially more aggressive in jailbreak identification (99 vs.\\ 52), suggesting systematic annotator bias. The low CP position agreement (mean absolute difference of 8.5 steps) supports the use of human-validated annotations in our primary analysis (HarmThoughts~\\citep{harmthoughts}) and motivates future investment in multi-annotator human CP labeling with formal IAA evaluation.\n\n\\section{Cross-Dataset CP Analysis: AdvBench Boundary Condition}\n\\label{app:advbench_cp}\n\nWe analyze R1-8B behavior on 520 AdvBench~\\citep{advbench} adversarial prompts to characterize a boundary condition for the CP framework (\\cref{tab:advbench_boundary}).\n\n\\begin{table}[h]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{R1-8B behavior on AdvBench adversarial prompts (520 total). This contrasts with HarmThoughts, where non-adversarial prompts trigger extended safety deliberation before commitment (mean relative CP position ${\\approx}0.7$). The CP framework is most informative when prompts elicit deliberation that can be monitored; adversarial prompts that circumvent deliberation represent a boundary condition where commitment detection is vacuous. ```text
472	\section{CP Annotation Perturbation}
473	\label{app:cp_perturbation}
474	
475	To assess sensitivity to annotation noise, we shift all ground-truth CP labels by $\delta \in \{-3, \ldots, +3\}$ steps and retrain the HS probe (\cref{tab:cp_perturbation}). Precision degrades smoothly with no cliff effect: within $\pm 2$ steps, BA changes by ${<}2.5$pp for all models (7--8B: ${<}1.0$pp). At the $\pm 3$-step worst case, all models retain precision well above their respective text baselines. These results support the annotation robustness assumption discussed in \cref{sec:limitations}.
476	
477	\begin{table}[h]
478	\centering
479	\small
480	\caption{HS probe sensitivity to CP annotation shift ($\delta$ steps). BA is stable across all perturbations; precision degrades gradually without cliff effects.}
481	\label{tab:cp_perturbation}
482	\begin{tabular}{llccc}
483	\toprule
484	\textbf{Model} & $\delta$ & \textbf{Prec} & \textbf{BA} & \textbf{FPR} \\
485	\midrule
486	R1-8B & $-3$ & .652 & .773 & .177 \\
487	 & $0$ & .596 & .784 & .165 \\
488	 & $+3$ & .530 & .771 & .143 \\[2pt]
489	OT-7B & $-3$ & .404 & .714 & .298 \\
490	 & $0$ & .351 & .707 & .286 \\
491	 & $+3$ & .300 & .702 & .276 \\[2pt]
492	QwQ-32B & $-3$ & .661 & .728 & .158 \\
493	 & $0$ & .609 & .690 & .129 \\
494	 & $+3$ & .550 & .676 & .114 \\[2pt]
495	R1-32B & $-3$ & .625 & .698 & .360 \\
496	 & $0$ & .570 & .722 & .281 \\
497	 & $+3$ & .503 & .750 & .236 \\
498	\bottomrule
499	\end{tabular}
500	\end{table}
501	
502	
503	\section{BOCPD/CUSUM Configuration Sweep}
504	\label{app:bocpd_config}
505	
506	We sweep hyperparameters for both Bayesian Online Change-Point Detection (BOCPD) and Cumulative Sum (CUSUM) methods applied to the temporal probe score sequences.
507	
508	\paragraph{BOCPD configurations.} BOCPD models the sequence of probe scores as piecewise-constant segments with Gaussian observation noise. The hazard rate $\lambda$ controls the prior probability of a change-point at each step ($P(\text{change}) = 1/\lambda$).
509	
510	\paragraph{CUSUM configurations.} CUSUM accumulates evidence for a positive mean shift. The drift parameter $\delta$ sets the minimum shift size to detect, and the threshold $h$ controls the detection boundary.
511	
512	\begin{table}[h]
513	\centering
514	\small
515	\caption{Change-point method configuration sweep on R1-8B. Detection rate is the fraction of jailbreak traces where the method fires. Lead is measured in steps before CP.}
516	\label{tab:cpd_sweep}
517	\setlength{\tabcolsep}{4pt}
518	\begin{tabular}{llcc}
519	\toprule
520	Method & Configuration & Det.\ Rate & Lead \\
521	\midrule
522	\multirow{5}{*}{BOCPD}
523	 & $\lambda = 5$   & 97 & +2.1 \\
524	 & $\lambda = 10$  & 93 & +3.5 \\
525	 & $\lambda = 20$  & 87 & +4.2 \\
526	 & $\lambda = 50$  & 72 & +5.8 \\
527	 & $\lambda = 100$ & 67.4 & +1.03 \\
528	\midrule
529	\multirow{10}{*}{CUSUM}
530	 & $\delta{=}0.3$, $h{=}3$ & 65.1 & +0.89 \\
531	 & $\delta{=}0.5$, $h{=}3$ & 89 & +1.4 \\
532	 & $\delta{=}0.5$, $h{=}5$ & 76 & +2.3 \\
533	 & $\delta{=}0.5$, $h{=}8$ & 61 & +3.1 \\
534	 & $\delta{=}1.0$, $h{=}3$ & 82 & +1.1 \\
535	 & $\delta{=}1.0$, $h{=}5$ & 68 & +1.9 \\
536	 & $\delta{=}1.0$, $h{=}8$ & 52 & +2.7 \\
537	 & $\delta{=}2.0$, $h{=}3$ & 71 & +0.8 \\
538	 & $\delta{=}2.0$, $h{=}5$ & 54 & +1.5 \\
539	 & $\delta{=}2.0$, $h{=}8$ & 38 & +2.1 \\
540	\midrule
541	Simple threshold & FCP baseline & 65.1 & +4.89 \\
542	\bottomrule
543	\end{tabular}
544	\end{table}
545	
546	The best BOCPD configuration ($\lambda{=}10$) achieves 93.0\% detection rate with +3.45 steps lead. The simple threshold baseline achieves 65.1\% detection with superior lead (+4.89 steps). All CUSUM configurations underperform the simple threshold on lead time.
547	
548	These results reveal a coverage-timing tradeoff. BOCPD ($\lambda{=}10$) achieves 28pp higher detection rate than simple thresholding (93.0\% vs.\ 65.1\%), but with lower lead (+3.45 vs.\ +4.89 steps). The transition toward unsafe reasoning is gradual rather than abrupt, so change-point methods cannot exploit discontinuities for early warning. When missed detections are costly, BOCPD's coverage advantage justifies its use despite the timing penalty. When early intervention is the priority, simple thresholding provides better lead time.
549	
550	\paragraph{Other models.} On OT-7B, QwQ-32B, and R1-32B, BOCPD achieves 0\% detection rate across all tested configurations ($\lambda \in \{5, 10, 50, 100, 200\}$). The failure modes differ by model: for R1-32B and QwQ-32B, probe scores transition gradually, so the hazard function never accumulates sufficient evidence; for OT-7B at $W{=}3$, transitions are too abrupt (0$\to$1 in 1--2 steps) for the Gaussian observation model to capture. R1-8B falls between these extremes, with transitions sharp enough to trigger the hazard function but smooth enough for the Gaussian model to track.
551	
552	
553	\section{Detection Threshold ($K$) Ablation}
554	\label{app:k_ablation}
555	
556	The consecutive-crossings threshold $K$ controls the trade-off between detection coverage and false positive rate. We evaluate $K \in \{1, 3, 5, 7, 10\}$ at $W{=}1$ on all models using HS probes at threshold-based layers (\cref{tab:k_ablation}).
```

      "filePath": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_5/projects/contrastive_trace_safety/docs/paper/sections/appendix.tex",
      "content": "\\section{CP Annotation Perturbation}\n\\label{app:cp_perturbation}\n\nTo assess sensitivity to annotation noise, we shift all ground-truth CP labels by $\\delta \\in \\{-3, \\ldots, +3\\}$ steps and retrain the HS probe (\\cref{tab:cp_perturbation}). Precision degrades smoothly with no cliff effect: within $\\pm 2$ steps, BA changes by ${<}2.5$pp for all models (7--8B: ${<}1.0$pp). At the $\\pm 3$-step worst case, all models retain precision well above their respective text baselines. These results support the annotation robustness assumption discussed in \\cref{sec:limitations}.\n\n\\begin{table}[h]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{HS probe sensitivity to CP annotation shift ($\\delta$ steps). BA is stable across all perturbations; precision degrades gradually without cliff effects.}\n\\label{tab:cp_perturbation}\n\\begin{tabular}{llccc}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Model} & $\\delta$ & \\textbf{Prec} & \\textbf{BA} & \\textbf{FPR} \\\\\n\\midrule\nR1-8B & $-3$ & .652 & .773 & .177 \\\\\n & $0$ & .596 & .784 & .165 \\\\\n & $+3$ & .530 & .771 & .143 \\\\[2pt]\nOT-7B & $-3$ & .404 & .714 & .298 \\\\\n & $0$ & .351 & .707 & .286 \\\\\n & $+3$ & .300 & .702 & .276 \\\\[2pt]\nQwQ-32B & $-3$ & .661 & .728 & .158 \\\\\n & $0$ & .609 & .690 & .129 \\\\\n & $+3$ & .550 & .676 & .114 \\\\[2pt]\nR1-32B & $-3$ & .625 & .698 & .360 \\\\\n & $0$ & .570 & .722 & .281 \\\\\n & $+3$ & .503 & .750 & .236 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\n\n\\section{BOCPD/CUSUM Configuration Sweep}\n\\label{app:bocpd_config}\n\nWe sweep hyperparameters for both Bayesian Online Change-Point Detection (BOCPD) and Cumulative Sum (CUSUM) methods applied to the temporal probe score sequences.\n\n\\paragraph{BOCPD configurations.} BOCPD models the sequence of probe scores as piecewise-constant segments with Gaussian observation noise. The hazard rate $\\lambda$ controls the prior probability of a change-point at each step ($P(\\text{change}) = 1/\\lambda$).\n\n\\paragraph{CUSUM configurations.} CUSUM accumulates evidence for a positive mean shift. The drift parameter $\\delta$ sets the minimum shift size to detect, and the threshold $h$ controls the detection boundary.\n\n\\begin{table}[h]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{Change-point method configuration sweep on R1-8B. Detection rate is the fraction of jailbreak traces where the method fires. Lead is measured in steps before CP.}\n\\label{tab:cpd_sweep}\n\\setlength{\\tabcolsep}{4pt}\n\\begin{tabular}{llcc}\n\\toprule\nMethod & Configuration & Det.\\ Rate & Lead \\\\\n\\midrule\n\\multirow{5}{*}{BOCPD}\n & $\\lambda = 5$   & 97 & +2.1 \\\\\n & $\\lambda = 10$  & 93 & +3.5 \\\\\n & $\\lambda = 20$  & 87 & +4.2 \\\\\n & $\\lambda = 50$  & 72 & +5.8 \\\\\n & $\\lambda = 100$ & 67.4 & +1.03 \\\\\n\\midrule\n\\multirow{10}{*}{CUSUM}\n & $\\delta{=}0.3$, $h{=}3$ & 65.1 & +0.89 \\\\\n & $\\delta{=}0.5$, $h{=}3$ & 89 & +1.4 \\\\\n & $\\delta{=}0.5$, $h{=}5$ & 76 & +2.3 \\\\\n & $\\delta{=}0.5$, $h{=}8$ & 61 & +3.1 \\\\\n & $\\delta{=}1.0$, $h{=}3$ & 82 & +1.1 \\\\\n & $\\delta{=}1.0$, $h{=}5$ & 68 & +1.9 \\\\\n & $\\delta{=}1.0$, $h{=}8$ & 52 & +2.7 \\\\\n & $\\delta{=}2.0$, $h{=}3$ & 71 & +0.8 \\\\\n & $\\delta{=}2.0$, $h{=}5$ & 54 & +1.5 \\\\\n & $\\delta{=}2.0$, $h{=}8$ & 38 & +2.1 \\\\\n\\midrule\nSimple threshold & FCP baseline & 65.1 & +4.89 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\nThe best BOCPD configuration ($\\lambda{=}10$) achieves 93.0\\% detection rate with +3.45 steps lead. The simple threshold baseline achieves 65.1\\% detection with superior lead (+4.89 steps). All CUSUM configurations underperform the simple threshold on lead time.\n\nThese results reveal a coverage-timing tradeoff. BOCPD ($\\lambda{=}10$) achieves 28pp higher detection rate than simple thresholding (93.0\\% vs.\\ 65.1\\%), but with lower lead (+3.45 vs.\\ +4.89 steps). The transition toward unsafe reasoning is gradual rather than abrupt, so change-point methods cannot exploit discontinuities for early warning. When missed detections are costly, BOCPD's coverage advantage justifies its use despite the timing penalty. When early intervention is the priority, simple thresholding provides better lead time.\n\n\\paragraph{Other models.} On OT-7B, QwQ-32B, and R1-32B, BOCPD achieves 0\\% detection rate across all tested configurations ($\\lambda \\in \\{5, 10, 50, 100, 200\\}$). The failure modes differ by model: for R1-32B and QwQ-32B, probe scores transition gradually, so the hazard function never accumulates sufficient evidence; for OT-7B at $W{=}3$, transitions are too abrupt (0$\\to$1 in 1--2 steps) for the Gaussian observation model to capture. R1-8B falls between these extremes, with transitions sharp enough to trigger the hazard function but smooth enough for the Gaussian model to track.\n\n\n\\section{Detection Threshold ($K$) Ablation}\n\\label{app:k_ablation}\n\nThe consecutive-crossings threshold $K$ controls the trade-off between detection coverage and false positive rate. We evaluate $K \\in \\{1, 3, 5, 7, 10\\}$ at $W{=}1$ on all models using HS probes at threshold-based layers (\\cref{tab:k_ablation}).",

    }

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_01BHsKM5TQPmg8D4ioXJ1CAa`</summary>

```text
146	Shallow layers produce highly compressed representations: consecutive steps cluster within small cosine distances (0.04--0.19), while deep-layer representations spread more widely (0.09--0.41). Inter-step distances at shallow layers are 1.4--2.7$\times$ smaller than at deep layers across all models. A linear probe operating in this compressed space sits near the noise floor, where minor numerical fluctuations produce spurious threshold crossings that inflate crossing rate independently of discriminative power. Deep layers produce well-separated representations where crossings correspond to genuine semantic transitions.
147	
148	\paragraph{Cross-dataset evidence.}
149	To assess whether this geometric precondition generalizes beyond HarmThoughts, we compute the same shallow/deep volatility ratio on AdvBench~\citep{advbench} traces using R1-8B. The deep-layer mean cosine distance (0.442) is $1.67\times$ the shallow-layer mean (0.265), consistent with the 1.4--2.7$\times$ range observed across all four models on HarmThoughts (\cref{tab:cosine_volatility}). This confirms that the representational compression underlying metric-induced layer selection bias is not dataset-specific and does not depend on CP annotations.
150	
151	
152	\section{Holm-Bonferroni Step-Down Correction}
153	\label{app:holm_bonferroni}
154	
155	\cref{tab:holm_bonferroni} reports the full test battery used to assess hidden-state superiority over text probes. Because balanced accuracy, precision, and FPR share the same confusion matrix, the three per-model metrics are correlated; the effective number of independent comparisons is four (one per model). We report both the conservative 12-test correction and the 4-test model-level correction: both yield the same outcome (all tests pass at $\alpha{=}0.05$). Under 4-test HB, the tightest threshold falls on R1-8B FPR ($p{=}0.044$, rank-4 threshold $\alpha/1{=}0.050$), which still passes. Tests are ranked by $p$-value; each test $i$ (rank $i$ of $m$) must satisfy $p_i < \alpha / (m - i + 1)$ under the step-down procedure.
156	
157	\begin{table}[h]

  "new_string": "This confirms that the representational compression underlying metric-induced layer selection bias is not dataset-specific and does not depend on CP annotations.\n\n\\subsection{AdvBench Boundary Condition}\n\\label{app:advbench_cp}\n\nWe analyze R1-8B behavior on 520 AdvBench~\\citep{advbench} adversarial prompts to characterize a boundary condition for the CP framework (\\cref{tab:advbench_boundary}).\n\n\\begin{table}[h]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{R1-8B behavior on AdvBench adversarial prompts (520 total). CP${=}0$ indicates the model produces harmful content from the first reasoning step without prior safety deliberation.}\n\\label{tab:advbench_boundary}\n\\begin{tabular}{lc}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Metric} & \\textbf{Value} \\\\\n\\midrule\nJailbreak rate & 13.4\\% \\\\\nCP${=}0$ (among JB) & 90.4\\% \\\\\nMean CP position (among JB) & 0.6 steps \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\nAdversarial prompts designed to bypass safety mechanisms (e.g., GCG suffixes) cause the model to skip safety deliberation entirely: 90.4\\% of jailbroken traces have CP${=}0$, meaning the model commits to harmful generation from the first reasoning step. This contrasts with HarmThoughts, where non-adversarial prompts trigger extended safety deliberation before commitment (mean relative CP position ${\\approx}0.7$). The CP framework is most informative when prompts elicit deliberation that can be monitored; adversarial prompts that circumvent deliberation represent a boundary condition where commitment detection is vacuous. The geometric compression evidence (\\cref{tab:cosine_volatility}; volatility ratio $1.67\\times$) remains valid because it measures representational properties independent of CP annotations.\n\n\\section{Holm-Bonferroni Step-Down Correction}"
}

_source line 3357 · 2026-05-20T07:45:33.384Z_

[Director 指令] [Patch] 两个新 Appendix section 需要写入，基于最新实验数据：

1. **Appendix: Inter-Annotator Agreement (IAA)**
- 数据：gpt-4o-mini vs Qwen3-32B 在 AdvBench 388 条 traces 上的标注对比
- Jailbreak 分类：Cohen's κ = 0.606 (moderate agreement)，gpt-4o-mini 判了 99 条 JB vs Qwen3 只判了 52 条
- CP 位置一致性：仅 37% exact match——两个 LLM 对"safety deliberation 何时停止"的理解根本不同
- 叙事：validates 论文使用人类标注（而非 LLM 标注）的方法选择。直接回应潜在 reviewer concern "为什么不用 LLM 自动标注 CP"
- 数据文件位置：`artifacts/advbench_cp_annotations/llm_cp_annotations_qwen3.json` 和 `llm_cp_annotations_gpt4omini.json`

2. **Appendix: AdvBench Boundary Condition**
- R1-8B 在 AdvBench（对抗性 jailbreak 提示）上的行为：520 条 traces，388 条 B-class，52 条 jailbreak (13.4%)，其中 47 条 CP=0（90.4%）
- 模型直接合规，完全跳过 safety deliberation——与 HarmThoughts（非对抗性提示触发深度 deliberation）行为模式根本不同
- 这定义了 CP 框架的适用边界：需要能触发 safety reasoning 的提示类型
- 已有的 volatility ratio 1.67×（几何证据）不受影响（已在论文中）
- 这是一个有意义的 negative result，不是失败

注意：严格 8 页主文限制，这两个 section 放 Appendix。编译后确认不影响页数。这次不用验证引用。

870	\end{tabular}
871	\caption{Position-residualized probe balanced accuracy. All residualized HS probes are above chance ($p{<}0.001$). 3/4 models retain significant non-positional HS advantage over text (gap +3.7 to +6.9pp, HB corrected); R1-32B is the exception (+1.9pp, $p{=}0.914$).}
872	\label{tab:position_residual}
873	\end{table}
874	
875	Position accounts for 9--20pp of balanced accuracy depending on model and window size (R1-32B's 20.4pp drop at $W{=}1$ reflects the absence of temporal context), and all models retain non-positional signal above chance ($p{<}0.001$). Three of four models retain significant non-positional HS advantage over text after residualization (OT-7B +6.9pp, R1-8B +6.4pp at $W{=}15$, QwQ-32B +3.7pp; all HB corrected). R1-32B is the exception: residualized HS-text gap is +1.9pp ($p{=}0.914$), a pattern consistent with position mediation. Larger temporal windows reduce position dependence: R1-8B drops 24.3pp at $W{=}3$ but only 16.5pp at $W{=}15$.
876	
877	For QwQ-32B specifically, three complementary significance tests confirm the residualized probe performs above chance: Wilcoxon signed-rank $p{=}0.0024$, paired $t$-test $p{=}0.0008$, permutation test $p{<}0.001$ (bootstrap 95\% CI for per-trace BA: $[0.575, 0.744]$). The position baseline achieves higher balanced accuracy (0.832 vs.\ HS 0.796), and HS precision (+4.5pp, $p{=}0.133$) and FPR (+2.5pp, $p{=}0.279$) margins over the position baseline are not statistically significant at $n{=}19$.
878	
879	\paragraph{Non-linear position terms.}
880	To test whether non-linear position effects drive the HS advantage, we repeat the residualization with both linear ($t/T$) and quadratic ($[t/T, (t/T)^2]$) regressors (\cref{tab:position_quadratic}). Both 7--8B models retain significant residualized advantages under either specification. R1-32B remains non-significant under both. QwQ-32B's residualized advantage survives linear control (+7.4pp, HB) but is fully absorbed by the quadratic term ($-$1.0pp), indicating a non-linear position component in its HS advantage.
881	
882	\begin{table}[h]
883	\centering
884	\small
885	\begin{tabular}{lcccc}
886	\toprule
887	\textbf{Model} & \textbf{Lin.\ gap} & \textbf{Quad.\ gap} & \textbf{Lin.\ HB} & \textbf{Quad.\ HB} \\
888	\midrule
889	R1-8B & +10.4pp & +10.3pp & \checkmark & \checkmark \\
890	OT-7B & +11.4pp & +9.8pp & \checkmark & \checkmark \\
891	R1-32B & $-$1.4pp & +0.0pp & $\times$ & $\times$ \\
892	QwQ-32B & +7.4pp & $-$1.0pp & \checkmark & $\times$ \\
893	\bottomrule
894	\end{tabular}
895	\caption{Linear vs.\ quadratic position residualization (residualized HS$-$text gap, L60 for QwQ-32B). Both 7--8B models are robust to either specification. QwQ-32B's advantage survives linear but not quadratic control; R1-32B is non-significant under both.}
896	\label{tab:position_quadratic}
897	\end{table}
898	
899	
900	\section{Hyperparameter Robustness}
901	\label{app:hyperparameter_robustness}
902	
903	The joint search space for layer (${\leq}64$), window $W$ (7 values), consecutive-crossing threshold $K$ (5 values), and decision threshold is large (up to 2{,}240 configurations per model). This raises the concern that reported performance reflects overfitting to small validation sets ($n{=}4$--$9$ per class at 32B). Three analyses address this concern.
904	
905	\paragraph{Threshold-based metrics converge to a deep-layer band.}
906	Despite the large search space, three of four threshold-based metrics (precision, inverse FPR, additive composite) independently select L63 (98\% depth) for both 32B models (\cref{tab:layer_selection}). A joint (layer, $W$) grid search over 192 configurations confirms that L63 is near-optimal for QwQ-32B at recommended windows (\cref{tab:joint_layer_w}). For R1-32B, layers L55--L63 at $W{=}1$ form a robust band where all 3/3 metrics pass Holm-Bonferroni (\cref{app:layer_sweep}). The convergence of independent metrics to a narrow band is inconsistent with overfitting, which would produce scattered selections.
907	
908	\paragraph{BA-optimal layer instability is the finding, not a confound.}
909	Five-fold cross-validation on 32B validation sets reveals that balanced-accuracy-optimal layers are highly unstable across folds (\cref{tab:fold_ba_layers}). This instability is not a limitation of the study but the central methodological finding: BA-layer selection is sensitive to data composition, and conclusions drawn from any single BA-optimal layer are unreliable. Despite per-fold instability, mean BA across folds peaks near L47--L50 for both models, consistent with the LOO-CV dominant layers (\cref{app:layer_sweep}). The HS advantage holds at both BA-optimal and precision-optimal layers regardless of depth (14/16 per-model tests pass HB; \cref{tab:ba_layer_comparison}).
910	
911	\begin{table}[h]
912	\centering
913	\small
914	\caption{BA-optimal layer per fold (5-fold CV, 32B models). Per-fold selections span 24 layers, but mean-BA landscape peaks are stable.}
915	\label{tab:fold_ba_layers}

917	\toprule

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_01CBRkPRBTZDkxpfwqrCyMKv`</summary>

```text
152	\label{app:advbench_cp}
153	
154	We analyze R1-8B behavior on 520 AdvBench~\citep{advbench} adversarial prompts to characterize a boundary condition for the CP framework (\cref{tab:advbench_boundary}).
155	
156	\begin{table}[h]
157	\centering
158	\small
159	\caption{R1-8B behavior on AdvBench adversarial prompts (520 total). CP${=}0$ indicates the model produces harmful content from the first reasoning step without prior safety deliberation.}
160	\label{tab:advbench_boundary}
161	\begin{tabular}{lc}
162	\toprule
163	\textbf{Metric} & \textbf{Value} \\
164	\midrule
165	Jailbreak rate & 13.4\% \\
166	CP${=}0$ (among JB) & 90.4\% \\
167	Mean CP position (among JB) & 0.6 steps \\
168	\bottomrule
169	\end{tabular}
170	\end{table}
171	
172	Adversarial prompts designed to bypass safety mechanisms (e.g., GCG suffixes) cause the model to skip safety deliberation entirely: 90.4\% of jailbroken traces have CP${=}0$, meaning the model commits to harmful generation from the first reasoning step. This contrasts with HarmThoughts, where non-adversarial prompts trigger extended safety deliberation before commitment (mean relative CP position ${\approx}0.7$). The CP framework is most informative when prompts elicit deliberation that can be monitored; adversarial prompts that circumvent deliberation represent a boundary condition where commitment detection is vacuous. The geometric compression evidence (\cref{tab:cosine_volatility}; volatility ratio $1.67\times$) remains valid because it measures representational properties independent of CP annotations.
173	
174	\section{Holm-Bonferroni Step-Down Correction}
175	\label{app:holm_bonferroni}
176	
```

```text
1	\section{Introduction}\label{sec:introduction}
2	
3	Reasoning models such as DeepSeek-R1~\cite{deepseekr1} and QwQ~\cite{qwq} scale inference-time computation through extended chains of thought (CoT). Recent work documents that these models can ``self-jailbreak'': without adversarial input modification, the CoT transitions from safety-aware deliberation to committed harmful generation~\cite{harmthoughts,selfjailbreak}. We define the first reasoning step after which safety deliberation permanently ceases as the \textbf{Safety Commitment Point} (CP). This failure mode differs qualitatively from adversarial jailbreaks on standard LLMs because the harmful transition originates within the model's own reasoning. Before retrospective characterization can inform future detection systems, a prior question must be answered: can current probing methods reliably distinguish pre-CP from post-CP reasoning steps, and under what conditions? This paper provides retrospective classification (not online detection) of what hidden-state and text-based probes can and cannot detect; false positive rates remain 11--50\%, so deployment applications are future work.\looseness=-1
4	
5	Existing safety detectors target fundamentally different problems. Hidden-state (HS) methods such as TrajGuard~\cite{trajguard} and SafeSwitch~\cite{safeswitch} classify adversarial inputs at the token level, while text monitors like SafeRemind~\cite{saferemind} scan generated reasoning for explicit violations. Neither tracks the gradual, multi-step commitment process. Before asking which modality is better, a prior methodological question must be answered: how sensitive are probe conclusions to the choice of layer selection metric? HS probes require choosing which transformer layer to monitor, a researcher degree of freedom whose metric sensitivity has received almost no scrutiny. We show this choice alone can reverse a study's central conclusion---the same model and data yield a 14-layer (R1-8B) to 61-layer (QwQ-32B) divergence depending solely on metric choice.\looseness=-1
6	
7	We present an initial characterization across four reasoning models---primarily Qwen-based (3/4 models), with one Llama model for limited cross-architecture evidence---at two scales (7--8B, 32B) on the HarmThoughts benchmark. Our central finding is methodological: the choice of layer selection metric entirely determines whether an HS advantage is observed. A composite metric (balanced accuracy $\times$ crossing rate) selects shallow layers for 32B models because shallow representations compress directional information, so a linear probe operates near the noise floor and threshold crossings reflect noise rather than semantic transitions. At these layers, only the two 7--8B models show significant HS advantage.\looseness=-1
8	
9	Threshold-based metrics (precision, inverse FPR, additive composite) share a common confusion matrix and empirically converge; BA independently confirms deep-layer selection (\cref{tab:layer_selection}). At these layers, the HS advantage is graded: \emph{cross-validated} at 7--8B (precision +20.1 to +21.9pp, nested CV; \cref{tab:hs_vs_text}), \emph{significant} but suggestive of position-competitiveness at QwQ-32B$^\dagger$ (+19.2pp; \cref{sec:32b-analysis}), and \emph{exploratory} at R1-32B$^\dagger$ (+15.4pp, pattern consistent with position mediation, below MDE; \cref{sec:experiments}).\looseness=-1
10	
11	Our contributions are:
12	\begin{itemize}
13	    \item \textbf{Metric-induced layer selection bias.} We identify a metric-induced systematic bias: crossing-rate composite metrics systematically select shallow layers across all four models (including Llama), reversing HS probe conclusions at 32B scale; a random-label control confirms the bias is geometric (\cref{sec:layer-results}).
14	
15	    \item \textbf{Encoder-quality boundary with evidence gradient.} Against 384d MiniLM, the HS advantage is cross-validated at 7--8B (+20.1 to +21.9pp, HB corrected, nested CV), significant but suggestive of position-competitiveness at QwQ-32B$^\dagger$ (+19.2pp), and exploratory at R1-32B$^\dagger$ (+15.4pp, pattern consistent with position mediation). Against 1024d BGE-large, only OT-7B survives correction (1/4 HB). PCA capacity matching preserves significance (\cref{sec:32b-analysis}).
16	
17	    \item \textbf{Negative results define method boundaries.} No alternative method consistently matches supervised probing at any evidence tier (\cref{tab:negative}): BOCPD/CUSUM fail on gradual transitions; Mahalanobis distance finds near-zero separation; RepE shows inconsistent performance across models (competitive on QwQ-32B, poor elsewhere); cross-model transfer fails under PCA alignment. Control task selectivity (11.1--16.8pp above chance; \cref{app:control}) confirms genuine safety structure.
18	\end{itemize}
19	
20	\noindent These findings provide initial characterization on one benchmark for hidden-state safety monitoring and identify layer selection as a critical confound that future work must address.\looseness=-1
21	
```

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_014oN6h1TTt7BtcaN18p6j95`</summary>

```text
1	\section{Related Work}\label{sec:related-work}
2	
3	\paragraph{Safety in Reasoning Models.}
4	Reasoning models introduce a qualitatively new failure mode: the model's own chain of thought can transition from safety-aware deliberation to harmful generation without adversarial input modification~\cite{wang2025lrmsafety}.
5	
6	\textit{Attack characterization.} Self-jailbreak has been documented with step-level annotations~\cite{harmthoughts}, with longer chains increasing circumvention probability~\cite{selfjailbreak}. H-CoT~\cite{hcot2025} shows reasoning chains can be externally hijacked. Unfaithful reasoning~\cite{chen2025unfaithful,turpin2023language} further motivates hidden-state monitoring that bypasses text. Safety-aligned models maintain refusal intentions during thinking but experience a sharp drop at output generation~\cite{yin2025refusal_cliff,hagendorff2026lrm_jailbreak,hu2026refusal_dynamics,ahrend2026safer_traces}.
7	
8	\textit{Detection.} CoT monitoring can detect reward hacking but optimization pressure promotes obfuscation~\cite{baker2025monitoring}. CoT monitorability is valuable but fragile~\cite{korbak2025cot_monitorability}. GuardReasoner~\cite{xiang2025guardreasoner} trains a dedicated reasoning-based guard model.
9	
10	\textit{Mitigation.} SafeThink~\cite{safethink} steers early reasoning toward safety, SafeRemind~\cite{saferemind} inserts entropy-triggered reminders, and Chain-of-Guardrails~\cite{mao2025selfjailbreak_reasoning} proposes guardrail insertion during reasoning. None of these conducts a systematic modality comparison for commitment detection.
11	
12	\paragraph{Hidden-State Safety Detection.}
13	Internal activations can detect safety violations in standard LLMs. \citet{azaria2023internal} demonstrate that internal states encode whether a model's output is truthful, motivating representation-based monitoring. \citet{zhou2024alignment} show safety-relevant information distributes across layers, while \citet{qi2025shallow_alignment} find that alignment operates primarily over early output tokens. \citet{arditi2024refusal} identify a single refusal direction, \citet{turner2023activation} steer behavior via activation addition without optimization, and \citet{zhao2025harmfulness} show harmfulness and refusal are encoded separately. \citet{joad2026refusal_multi} reveal eleven category-specific refusal directions, \citet{templeton2024scaling} extract interpretable features at scale, and \citet{wollschlager2025geometry} formalize multi-dimensionality through concept cones, consistent with our finding that single-direction methods are insufficient. TrajGuard~\cite{trajguard} uses Mahalanobis distance from safe trajectories; SafeSwitch~\cite{safeswitch} trains hidden-state classifiers; SIREN~\cite{siren} monitors internal representations during generation; SafeDream~\cite{safedream2026} proposes proactive world models for detection; HiddenDetect~\cite{jiang2025hiddendetect} extends hidden-state monitoring to vision-language models; SafeInt~\cite{safeint2025} applies representation intervention for defense; RCS~\cite{rcs2026} uses Mahalanobis-based contrastive scoring for jailbreak detection. These methods assume threats from external input manipulation and operate at the token level on instruction-tuned models. The self-jailbreak setting differs because transitions are internal and span multiple reasoning steps. \citet{kadali2025internal} decompose internal layer representations to separate jailbreak from benign prompts; their results confirm that layer-wise structure carries safety-relevant signal. Concurrently, \citet{chan2025probing} probe safety commitment in LLMs, complementing our focus on metric-induced layer selection bias. We reproduce TrajGuard (\cref{sec:negative}) and find its geometric approach fails because discriminative signal resides in minor PCA components.
14	
15	\paragraph{Probing Methodology and Layer Selection.}
16	Causal abstraction~\cite{geiger2025causal} formalizes when high-level causal models faithfully describe neural computation, grounding use of internal representations. Linear probing spans layer-wise classifiers~\cite{alain2017understanding}, sentence probing~\cite{conneau2018probing}, and surveys~\cite{belinkov2019analysis,belinkov2022probing,rogers2020primer}. Control tasks~\cite{hewitt2019control} distinguish structure from memorization (we adopt these; \cref{app:control}). Information-theoretic and causal formulations address probe complexity~\cite{voita2020information,pimentel2020information,ravfogel2020null,ravfogel2022rlace,belrose2023leace,elazar2021amnesic}. \citet{marks2024geometry} find emergent linear structure in LLM truth representations, consistent with our safety commitment separability. That information distributes non-uniformly across layers is well established~\cite{tenney2019bert,jawahar2019bert}. Our contribution is orthogonal: the known layer-wise information gradient interacts with metric choice to produce systematic selection bias in safety probes. Most directly, \citet{boxo2025linear_probes} show linear probes can rely on textual evidence rather than deeper representations; our metric bias analysis provides a complementary mechanism: shallow-layer probes exploit compressed representations that inflate crossing rate, a geometric effect consistent with text-leakage at layers where representations have not yet diverged from surface features. \citet{alvarez2026transport_geometry} apply hidden-state geometry to step-level hallucination detection concurrently with our work. Despite this foundation, layer selection remains underexamined: most studies select by a single metric without sensitivity analysis, a practice we show can reverse conclusions (\cref{sec:layer-results}). \citet{pais2026linear_probe_scaling} show that multi-layer ensembling recovers performance where single-layer probes fail, a complementary solution to the sensitivity problem we diagnose. Our approach is correlational; causal validation~\citep{geiger2025causal,belrose2023leace,li2024iti} is needed to confirm probed directions are causally active.
17	
18	\paragraph{Alternative Detection Approaches.}
19	BOCPD~\cite{adams2007bayesian} detects abrupt distributional shifts; CCS~\cite{burns2023discovering} discovers latent truth directions via consistency constraints; RepE~\cite{zou2023representation} identifies concept directions through mean differences. We adapt all three to temporal commitment detection (\cref{sec:negative}). BOCPD and CUSUM fail because commitment is gradual. CCS provides no gain because its consistency objective misaligns with temporal structure. RepE shows inconsistent performance across models (0.756 on QwQ, 0.559 on R1-32B). These failures motivate supervised probes with principled layer selection as the minimal effective methodology.
20	
```

We adapt all three to temporal commitment detection (\\cref{sec:negative}). BOCPD and CUSUM fail because commitment is gradual. CCS provides no gain because its consistency objective misaligns with temporal structure. RepE shows inconsistent performance across models (0.756 on QwQ, 0.559 on R1-32B). These failures motivate supervised probes with principled layer selection as the minimal effective methodology.\n",
      "numLines": 20,
      "startLine": 1,
      "totalLines": 20
    }
  },

        "content": "\\section{Problem Setup and Methods}\n\\label{sec:method}\n\n\\subsection{Safety Commitment Point}\n\\label{sec:commitment-point}\n\nWhen a reasoning model is jailbroken, its chain-of-thought (CoT) transitions from safety-aware deliberation to committed harmful generation. We define the \\textbf{Safety Commitment Point} (CP) as the first reasoning step after which safety reasoning permanently ceases; traces with oscillating safety deliberation are resolved by taking the final irreversible transition. This binary formulation is a deliberate simplification; we discuss its limits in \\cref{sec:limitations}. Steps before CP are \\emph{pre-commitment} ($y_t = 0$); steps at or after CP are \\emph{post-commitment} ($y_t = 1$). The classification task produces a score $s_t \\in [0, 1]$ estimating whether the model has passed its CP by step $t$.\n\nWe study four reasoning models spanning two architectures and two scales (\\cref{tab:models}).\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\resizebox{\\columnwidth}{!}{%\n\\begin{tabular}{lccccc}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Model} & \\textbf{Arch.} & \\textbf{Params} & \\textbf{Layers} & \\textbf{Hidden} & \\textbf{$n$ (total\\,/\\,test)} \\\\\n\\midrule\nR1-8B & Llama & 8B & 32 & 4096 & 209\\,/\\,43 \\\\\nOT-7B & Qwen & 7B & 28 & 3584 & 228\\,/\\,47 \\\\\nR1-32B & Qwen & 32B & 64 & 5120 & 100\\,/\\,20 \\\\\nQwQ-32B & Qwen & 32B & 64 & 5120 & 95\\,/\\,19 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}}\n\\caption{Models studied. R1-8B and R1-32B are distilled from DeepSeek-R1~\\citep{deepseekr1}; QwQ-32B~\\citep{qwq} is a native reasoning model; OT-7B~\\citep{openthinker} is trained with STILL~\\citep{still} on Qwen2.5-7B. Data: HarmThoughts~\\citep{harmthoughts} with step-level CP annotations. Split: 60/20/20.}\n\\label{tab:models}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\subsection{Probe Architecture}\n\\label{sec:probe-architecture}\n\n\\paragraph{Hidden-state (HS) probes.} At each step $t$, we extract the last-token hidden state $\\mathbf{h}_t^{(\\ell)} \\in \\mathbb{R}^d$ from layer $\\ell$ and train an L2-regularized logistic regression classifier (balanced class weights, $C{=}1.0$, LBFGS, max\\_iter${}=2000$) on binary CP labels. Sweeping $C \\in [0.01, 100]$ yields $<$2pp BA variation (\\cref{sec:experiments}), confirming robustness to this default. Layer selection is analyzed in \\cref{sec:layer-sensitivity}.\n\n\\paragraph{Text probes.} Each step's text is encoded with all-MiniLM-L6-v2~\\citep{wang2020minilm} ($\\mathbb{R}^{384}$), a distilled sentence encoder from the Sentence-BERT family~\\citep{reimers2019sentence}. The same logistic regression is applied as a surface-level modality baseline.\n\n\\paragraph{Temporal windowing.} For window size $W$, we concatenate features from steps $[t{-}W{+}1, \\ldots, t]$. We evaluate $W \\in \\{1, 3, 5, 10, 15, 20, 25\\}$ and select per-model on validation. GRU sequence models are also evaluated under two protocols: a 70/30 train/test split (primary) and the same 60/20/20 split used for LR experiments (for fair comparison; \\cref{tab:gru_multi_seeds,app:gru_details}).\n\n\\subsection{Layer Selection Sensitivity}\n\\label{sec:layer-sensitivity}\n\nLayer selection is a researcher degree of freedom that can reverse modality comparison conclusions. We evaluate five metrics: balanced accuracy (BA), precision, and inverse FPR are standard probing metrics~\\citep{belinkov2022probing}; crossing rate formalizes cross-layer prediction disagreement~\\citep{kaya2019shallow,zhou2020bert}, and additive composite. A multiplicative composite (BA $\\times$ crossing rate) serves as the \\emph{crossing-rate composite} (CR-composite) baseline, so named because it incorporates crossing rate in the selection criterion. Metrics evaluating probe performance at a fixed decision threshold (precision, inverse FPR, additive composite) form the \\emph{threshold-based} selection. This classification is an empirical observation from metric disagreement, not an a priori design choice. Crossing rate inflates at shallow layers where representations are compressed (inter-step cosine distances 1.4--2.7$\\times$ smaller; \\cref{app:cosine_volatility}), an independently verifiable geometric property. Precision, inverse FPR, and additive composite all derive from the same $2\\times2$ confusion matrix and empirically converge to the same layer, consistent with their shared structure but not mathematically guaranteed; BA is threshold-free and provides independent confirmation. The ablation diagnoses which \\emph{aspect} drives layer selection bias. The effective number of independent tests is four (one per model), reflected in our HB correction. Results in \\cref{sec:layer-results}; full per-layer sweeps in \\cref{app:layer_sweep}.\n\n\\subsection{Baseline Methods}\n\\label{sec:baselines}\n\nWe evaluate seven alternatives to contextualize probe performance (\\cref{tab:negative}): BOCPD~\\citep{adams2007bayesian} and CUSUM~\\citep{page1954cusum} for change-point detection; Mahalanobis distance following TrajGuard~\\citep{trajguard}; RepE mean-difference directions~\\citep{zou2023representation}; two-layer MLP probes; CCS-adapted~\\citep{burns2023discovering}; and cross-model transfer via PCA. A position-only baseline ($t/T$) controls for temporal artifacts. Detailed configurations in \\cref{app:bocpd_config,app:transfer_details}.\n\n\\subsection{Evaluation Protocol}\n\\label{sec:evaluation}\n\nWe report balanced accuracy, precision, pre-CP FPR, coverage, and lead time. We exclude F1 (conflates precision and recall), AUROC (threshold-free, cannot diagnose layer selection bias), and MCC (redundant with BA under balanced priors). Positive classification requires $K{=}5$ consecutive threshold crossings, selected on the R1-8B validation set and held fixed across all models as the value balancing coverage ($\\geq$70\\%) and safe-trace false positive rate ($\\leq$50\\%); \\cref{tab:k_ablation} confirms that the HS vs.\\ text conclusion direction is consistent at $K{=}3$, $5$, and $7$, with coverage and FPR trading off monotonically. Modality comparisons use BCa bootstrap (10{,}000 resamples, resampling traces as clusters: each trace is an independent unit and all its steps are included or excluded together, preserving within-trace dependence)~\\citep{efron1993bootstrap,diciccio1996bootstrap} with Holm-Bonferroni step-down (3 metrics $\\times$ 4 models $= 12$ tests, $\\alpha{=}0.05$); the 4-test framework yields identical conclusions (\\cref{app:holm_bonferroni}). For 32B models ($n \\leq 20$), the exact permutation test is the primary inference method; BCa confidence intervals are reported for comparability but may have reduced coverage at these sample sizes (BCa assumes approximate pivotality; heavy-tailed score distributions could produce anti-conservative intervals). Within each model, the three metrics are positively correlated, so the correction is stricter than under independence. Control task selectivity~\\citep{hewitt2019control} validates genuine structure (\\cref{app:control}). $\\dagger$ marks sample-limited test sets ($n \\leq 20$): R1-32B ($n{=}20$) and QwQ-32B ($n{=}19$).\n",
        "numLines": 51,
        "startLine": 1,
        "totalLines": 51
      }
    },

_source line 3490 · 2026-05-20T15:11:29.665Z_

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_0178eSZphxhwhjELbMb8M9PX`</summary>

```text
1	\section{Introduction}\label{sec:introduction}
2	
3	Reasoning models such as DeepSeek-R1~\cite{deepseekr1} and QwQ~\cite{qwq} scale inference-time computation through extended chains of thought (CoT). Recent work documents that these models can ``self-jailbreak'': without adversarial input modification, the CoT transitions from safety-aware deliberation to committed harmful generation~\cite{harmthoughts,selfjailbreak}. We define the first reasoning step after which safety deliberation permanently ceases as the \textbf{Safety Commitment Point} (CP). This failure mode differs qualitatively from adversarial jailbreaks on standard LLMs because the harmful transition originates within the model's own reasoning. Before retrospective characterization can inform future detection systems, a prior question must be answered: can current probing methods reliably distinguish pre-CP from post-CP reasoning steps, and under what conditions? This paper provides retrospective classification (not online detection) of what hidden-state and text-based probes can and cannot detect; false positive rates remain 11--50\%, so deployment applications are future work.\looseness=-1
4	
5	Existing safety detectors target fundamentally different problems. Hidden-state (HS) methods such as TrajGuard~\cite{trajguard} and SafeSwitch~\cite{safeswitch} classify adversarial inputs at the token level, while text monitors like SafeRemind~\cite{saferemind} scan generated reasoning for explicit violations. Neither tracks the gradual, multi-step commitment process. Before asking which modality is better, a prior methodological question must be answered: how sensitive are probe conclusions to the choice of layer selection metric? HS probes require choosing which transformer layer to monitor, a researcher degree of freedom whose metric sensitivity has received almost no scrutiny. We show this choice alone can reverse a study's central conclusion---the same model and data yield a 14-layer (R1-8B) to 61-layer (QwQ-32B) divergence depending solely on metric choice.\looseness=-1
6	
7	We present an initial characterization across four reasoning models---primarily Qwen-based (3/4 models), with one Llama model for limited cross-architecture evidence---at two scales (7--8B, 32B) on the HarmThoughts benchmark. Our central finding is methodological: the choice of layer selection metric entirely determines whether an HS advantage is observed. A composite metric (balanced accuracy $\times$ crossing rate) selects shallow layers for 32B models because shallow representations compress directional information, so a linear probe operates near the noise floor and threshold crossings reflect noise rather than semantic transitions. At these layers, only the two 7--8B models show significant HS advantage.\looseness=-1
8	
9	Threshold-based metrics (precision, inverse FPR, additive composite) share a common confusion matrix and empirically converge; BA independently confirms deep-layer selection (\cref{tab:layer_selection}). At these layers, the HS advantage is graded: \emph{cross-validated} at 7--8B (precision +20.1 to +21.9pp, nested CV; \cref{tab:hs_vs_text}), \emph{significant} but suggestive of position-competitiveness at QwQ-32B$^\dagger$ (+19.2pp; \cref{sec:32b-analysis}), and \emph{exploratory} at R1-32B$^\dagger$ (+15.4pp, pattern consistent with position mediation, below MDE; \cref{sec:experiments}).\looseness=-1
10	
11	Our contributions are:
12	\begin{itemize}
13	    \item \textbf{Metric-induced layer selection bias.} We identify a metric-induced systematic bias: crossing-rate composite metrics systematically select shallow layers across all four models (including Llama), reversing HS probe conclusions at 32B scale; a random-label control confirms the bias is geometric (\cref{sec:layer-results}).
14	
15	    \item \textbf{Encoder-quality boundary with evidence gradient.} Against 384d MiniLM, the HS advantage is cross-validated at 7--8B (+20.1 to +21.9pp, HB corrected, nested CV), significant but suggestive of position-competitiveness at QwQ-32B$^\dagger$ (+19.2pp), and exploratory at R1-32B$^\dagger$ (+15.4pp, pattern consistent with position mediation). Against 1024d BGE-large, only OT-7B survives correction (1/4 HB). PCA capacity matching preserves significance (\cref{sec:32b-analysis}).
16	
17	    \item \textbf{Negative results define method boundaries.} No alternative method consistently matches supervised probing at any evidence tier (\cref{tab:negative}): BOCPD/CUSUM fail on gradual transitions; Mahalanobis distance finds near-zero separation; RepE shows inconsistent performance across models (competitive on QwQ-32B, poor elsewhere); cross-model transfer fails under PCA alignment. Control task selectivity (11.1--16.8pp above chance; \cref{app:control}) confirms genuine safety structure.
18	\end{itemize}
19	
20	\noindent These findings provide initial characterization on one benchmark for hidden-state safety monitoring and identify layer selection as a critical confound that future work must address.\looseness=-1
21	
```

      "content": "\\section{Introduction}\\label{sec:introduction}\n\nReasoning models such as DeepSeek-R1~\\cite{deepseekr1} and QwQ~\\cite{qwq} scale inference-time computation through extended chains of thought (CoT). Recent work documents that these models can ``self-jailbreak'': without adversarial input modification, the CoT transitions from safety-aware deliberation to committed harmful generation~\\cite{harmthoughts,selfjailbreak}. We define the first reasoning step after which safety deliberation permanently ceases as the \\textbf{Safety Commitment Point} (CP). This failure mode differs qualitatively from adversarial jailbreaks on standard LLMs because the harmful transition originates within the model's own reasoning. Before retrospective characterization can inform future detection systems, a prior question must be answered: can current probing methods reliably distinguish pre-CP from post-CP reasoning steps, and under what conditions? This paper provides retrospective classification (not online detection) of what hidden-state and text-based probes can and cannot detect; false positive rates remain 11--50\\%, so deployment applications are future work.\\looseness=-1\n\nExisting safety detectors target fundamentally different problems. Hidden-state (HS) methods such as TrajGuard~\\cite{trajguard} and SafeSwitch~\\cite{safeswitch} classify adversarial inputs at the token level, while text monitors like SafeRemind~\\cite{saferemind} scan generated reasoning for explicit violations. Neither tracks the gradual, multi-step commitment process. Before asking which modality is better, a prior methodological question must be answered: how sensitive are probe conclusions to the choice of layer selection metric? HS probes require choosing which transformer layer to monitor, a researcher degree of freedom whose metric sensitivity has received almost no scrutiny. We show this choice alone can reverse a study's central conclusion---the same model and data yield a 14-layer (R1-8B) to 61-layer (QwQ-32B) divergence depending solely on metric choice.\\looseness=-1\n\nWe present an initial characterization across four reasoning models---primarily Qwen-based (3/4 models), with one Llama model for limited cross-architecture evidence---at two scales (7--8B, 32B) on the HarmThoughts benchmark. Our central finding is methodological: the choice of layer selection metric entirely determines whether an HS advantage is observed. A composite metric (balanced accuracy $\\times$ crossing rate) selects shallow layers for 32B models because shallow representations compress directional information, so a linear probe operates near the noise floor and threshold crossings reflect noise rather than semantic transitions. At these layers, only the two 7--8B models show significant HS advantage.\\looseness=-1\n\nThreshold-based metrics (precision, inverse FPR, additive composite) share a common confusion matrix and empirically converge; BA independently confirms deep-layer selection (\\cref{tab:layer_selection}). At these layers, the HS advantage is graded: \\emph{cross-validated} at 7--8B (precision +20.1 to +21.9pp, nested CV; \\cref{tab:hs_vs_text}), \\emph{significant} but suggestive of position-competitiveness at QwQ-32B$^\\dagger$ (+19.2pp; \\cref{sec:32b-analysis}), and \\emph{exploratory} at R1-32B$^\\dagger$ (+15.4pp, pattern consistent with position mediation, below MDE; \\cref{sec:experiments}).\\looseness=-1\n\nOur contributions are:\n\\begin{itemize}\n    \\item \\textbf{Metric-induced layer selection bias.} We identify a metric-induced systematic bias: crossing-rate composite metrics systematically select shallow layers across all four models (including Llama), reversing HS probe conclusions at 32B scale; a random-label control confirms the bias is geometric (\\cref{sec:layer-results}).\n\n    \\item \\textbf{Encoder-quality boundary with evidence gradient.} Against 384d MiniLM, the HS advantage is cross-validated at 7--8B (+20.1 to +21.9pp, HB corrected, nested CV), significant but suggestive of position-competitiveness at QwQ-32B$^\\dagger$ (+19.2pp), and exploratory at R1-32B$^\\dagger$ (+15.4pp, pattern consistent with position mediation). Against 1024d BGE-large, only OT-7B survives correction (1/4 HB). PCA capacity matching preserves significance (\\cref{sec:32b-analysis}).\n\n    \\item \\textbf{Negative results define method boundaries.} No alternative method consistently matches supervised probing at any evidence tier (\\cref{tab:negative}): BOCPD/CUSUM fail on gradual transitions; Mahalanobis distance finds near-zero separation; RepE shows inconsistent performance across models (competitive on QwQ-32B, poor elsewhere); cross-model transfer fails under PCA alignment. Control task selectivity (11.1--16.8pp above chance; \\cref{app:control}) confirms genuine safety structure.\n\\end{itemize}\n\n\\noindent These findings provide initial characterization on one benchmark for hidden-state safety monitoring and identify layer selection as a critical confound that future work must address.\\looseness=-1\n",
      "numLines": 21,
      "startLine": 1,
      "totalLines": 21
    }
  },
  "sourceToolAssistantUUID": "4ed4602b-e25a-4852-bffd-d8f0eb82b0fd",

```text
1	\section{Related Work}\label{sec:related-work}
2	
3	\paragraph{Safety in Reasoning Models.}
4	Reasoning models introduce a qualitatively new failure mode: the model's own chain of thought can transition from safety-aware deliberation to harmful generation without adversarial input modification~\cite{wang2025lrmsafety}.
5	
6	\textit{Attack characterization.} Self-jailbreak has been documented with step-level annotations~\cite{harmthoughts}, with longer chains increasing circumvention probability~\cite{selfjailbreak}. H-CoT~\cite{hcot2025} shows reasoning chains can be externally hijacked. Unfaithful reasoning~\cite{chen2025unfaithful,turpin2023language} further motivates hidden-state monitoring that bypasses text. Safety-aligned models maintain refusal intentions during thinking but experience a sharp drop at output generation~\cite{yin2025refusal_cliff,hagendorff2026lrm_jailbreak,hu2026refusal_dynamics,ahrend2026safer_traces}.
7	
8	\textit{Detection.} CoT monitoring can detect reward hacking but optimization pressure promotes obfuscation~\cite{baker2025monitoring}. CoT monitorability is valuable but fragile~\cite{korbak2025cot_monitorability}. GuardReasoner~\cite{xiang2025guardreasoner} trains a dedicated reasoning-based guard model.
9	
10	\textit{Mitigation.} SafeThink~\cite{safethink} steers early reasoning toward safety, SafeRemind~\cite{saferemind} inserts entropy-triggered reminders, and Chain-of-Guardrails~\cite{mao2025selfjailbreak_reasoning} proposes guardrail insertion during reasoning. None of these conducts a systematic modality comparison for commitment detection.
11	
12	\paragraph{Hidden-State Safety Detection.}
13	Internal activations can detect safety violations in standard LLMs. \citet{azaria2023internal} demonstrate that internal states encode whether a model's output is truthful, motivating representation-based monitoring. \citet{zhou2024alignment} show safety-relevant information distributes across layers, while \citet{qi2025shallow_alignment} find that alignment operates primarily over early output tokens. \citet{arditi2024refusal} identify a single refusal direction, \citet{turner2023activation} steer behavior via activation addition without optimization, and \citet{zhao2025harmfulness} show harmfulness and refusal are encoded separately. \citet{joad2026refusal_multi} reveal eleven category-specific refusal directions, \citet{templeton2024scaling} extract interpretable features at scale, and \citet{wollschlager2025geometry} formalize multi-dimensionality through concept cones, consistent with our finding that single-direction methods are insufficient. TrajGuard~\cite{trajguard} uses Mahalanobis distance from safe trajectories; SafeSwitch~\cite{safeswitch} trains hidden-state classifiers; SIREN~\cite{siren} monitors internal representations during generation; SafeDream~\cite{safedream2026} proposes proactive world models for detection; HiddenDetect~\cite{jiang2025hiddendetect} extends hidden-state monitoring to vision-language models; SafeInt~\cite{safeint2025} applies representation intervention for defense; RCS~\cite{rcs2026} uses Mahalanobis-based contrastive scoring for jailbreak detection. These methods assume threats from external input manipulation and operate at the token level on instruction-tuned models. The self-jailbreak setting differs because transitions are internal and span multiple reasoning steps. \citet{kadali2025internal} decompose internal layer representations to separate jailbreak from benign prompts; their results confirm that layer-wise structure carries safety-relevant signal. Concurrently, \citet{chan2025probing} probe safety commitment in LLMs, complementing our focus on metric-induced layer selection bias. We reproduce TrajGuard (\cref{sec:negative}) and find its geometric approach fails because discriminative signal resides in minor PCA components.
14	
15	\paragraph{Probing Methodology and Layer Selection.}
16	Causal abstraction~\cite{geiger2025causal} formalizes when high-level causal models faithfully describe neural computation, grounding use of internal representations. Linear probing spans layer-wise classifiers~\cite{alain2017understanding}, sentence probing~\cite{conneau2018probing}, and surveys~\cite{belinkov2019analysis,belinkov2022probing,rogers2020primer}. Control tasks~\cite{hewitt2019control} distinguish structure from memorization (we adopt these; \cref{app:control}). Information-theoretic and causal formulations address probe complexity~\cite{voita2020information,pimentel2020information,ravfogel2020null,ravfogel2022rlace,belrose2023leace,elazar2021amnesic}. \citet{marks2024geometry} find emergent linear structure in LLM truth representations, consistent with our safety commitment separability. That information distributes non-uniformly across layers is well established~\cite{tenney2019bert,jawahar2019bert}. Our contribution is orthogonal: the known layer-wise information gradient interacts with metric choice to produce systematic selection bias in safety probes. Most directly, \citet{boxo2025linear_probes} show linear probes can rely on textual evidence rather than deeper representations; our metric bias analysis provides a complementary mechanism: shallow-layer probes exploit compressed representations that inflate crossing rate, a geometric effect consistent with text-leakage at layers where representations have not yet diverged from surface features. \citet{alvarez2026transport_geometry} apply hidden-state geometry to step-level hallucination detection concurrently with our work. Despite this foundation, layer selection remains underexamined: most studies select by a single metric without sensitivity analysis, a practice we show can reverse conclusions (\cref{sec:layer-results}). \citet{pais2026linear_probe_scaling} show that multi-layer ensembling recovers performance where single-layer probes fail, a complementary solution to the sensitivity problem we diagnose. Our approach is correlational; causal validation~\citep{geiger2025causal,belrose2023leace,li2024iti} is needed to confirm probed directions are causally active.
17	
18	\paragraph{Alternative Detection Approaches.}
19	BOCPD~\cite{adams2007bayesian} detects abrupt distributional shifts; CCS~\cite{burns2023discovering} discovers latent truth directions via consistency constraints; RepE~\cite{zou2023representation} identifies concept directions through mean differences. We adapt all three to temporal commitment detection (\cref{sec:negative}). BOCPD and CUSUM fail because commitment is gradual. CCS provides no gain because its consistency objective misaligns with temporal structure. RepE shows inconsistent performance across models (0.756 on QwQ, 0.559 on R1-32B). These failures motivate supervised probes with principled layer selection as the minimal effective methodology.
20	
```

More broadly, no prior work tracks how safety signals evolve step-by-step during extended reasoning; existing hidden-state analyses operate across layers (spatial) rather than across generation steps (temporal).\\looseness=-1",
    "originalFile": "\\section{Introduction}\\label{sec:introduction}\n\nReasoning models such as DeepSeek-R1~\\cite{deepseekr1} and QwQ~\\cite{qwq} scale inference-time computation through extended chains of thought (CoT). Recent work documents that these models can ``self-jailbreak'': without adversarial input modification, the CoT transitions from safety-aware deliberation to committed harmful generation~\\cite{harmthoughts,selfjailbreak}. We define the first reasoning step after which safety deliberation permanently ceases as the \\textbf{Safety Commitment Point} (CP). This failure mode differs qualitatively from adversarial jailbreaks on standard LLMs because the harmful transition originates within the model's own reasoning. Before retrospective characterization can inform future detection systems, a prior question must be answered: can current probing methods reliably distinguish pre-CP from post-CP reasoning steps, and under what conditions? This paper provides retrospective classification (not online detection) of what hidden-state and text-based probes can and cannot detect; false positive rates remain 11--50\\%, so deployment applications are future work.\\looseness=-1\n\nExisting safety detectors target fundamentally different problems. Hidden-state (HS) methods such as TrajGuard~\\cite{trajguard} and SafeSwitch~\\cite{safeswitch} classify adversarial inputs at the token level, while text monitors like SafeRemind~\\cite{saferemind} scan generated reasoning for explicit violations. Neither tracks the gradual, multi-step commitment process. Before asking which modality is better, a prior methodological question must be answered: how sensitive are probe conclusions to the choice of layer selection metric? HS probes require choosing which transformer layer to monitor, a researcher degree of freedom whose metric sensitivity has received almost no scrutiny. We show this choice alone can reverse a study's central conclusion---the same model and data yield a 14-layer (R1-8B) to 61-layer (QwQ-32B) divergence depending solely on metric choice.\\looseness=-1\n\nWe present an initial characterization across four reasoning models---primarily Qwen-based (3/4 models), with one Llama model for limited cross-architecture evidence---at two scales (7--8B, 32B) on the HarmThoughts benchmark. Our central finding is methodological: the choice of layer selection metric entirely determines whether an HS advantage is observed. A composite metric (balanced accuracy $\\times$ crossing rate) selects shallow layers for 32B models because shallow representations compress directional information, so a linear probe operates near the noise floor and threshold crossings reflect noise rather than semantic transitions. At these layers, only the two 7--8B models show significant HS advantage.\\looseness=-1\n\nThreshold-based metrics (precision, inverse FPR, additive composite) share a common confusion matrix and empirically converge; BA independently confirms deep-layer selection (\\cref{tab:layer_selection}). At these layers, the HS advantage is graded: \\emph{cross-validated} at 7--8B (precision +20.1 to +21.9pp, nested CV; \\cref{tab:hs_vs_text}), \\emph{significant} but suggestive of position-competitiveness at QwQ-32B$^\\dagger$ (+19.2pp; \\cref{sec:32b-analysis}), and \\emph{exploratory} at R1-32B$^\\dagger$ (+15.4pp, pattern consistent with position mediation, below MDE; \\cref{sec:experiments}).\\looseness=-1\n\nOur contributions are:\n\\begin{itemize}\n    \\item \\textbf{Metric-induced layer selection bias.} We identify a metric-induced systematic bias: crossing-rate composite metrics systematically select shallow layers across all four models (including Llama), reversing HS probe conclusions at 32B scale; a random-label control confirms the bias is geometric (\\cref{sec:layer-results}).\n\n    \\item \\textbf{Encoder-quality boundary with evidence gradient.} Against 384d MiniLM, the HS advantage is cross-validated at 7--8B (+20.1 to +21.9pp, HB corrected, nested CV), significant but suggestive of position-competitiveness at QwQ-32B$^\\dagger$ (+19.2pp), and exploratory at R1-32B$^\\dagger$ (+15.4pp, pattern consistent with position mediation). Against 1024d BGE-large, only OT-7B survives correction (1/4 HB). PCA capacity matching preserves significance (\\cref{sec:32b-analysis}).\n\n    \\item \\textbf{Negative results define method boundaries.} No alternative method consistently matches supervised probing at any evidence tier (\\cref{tab:negative}): BOCPD/CUSUM fail on gradual transitions; Mahalanobis distance finds near-zero separation; RepE shows inconsistent performance across models (competitive on QwQ-32B, poor elsewhere); cross-model transfer fails under PCA alignment. Control task selectivity (11.1--16.8pp above chance; \\cref{app:control}) confirms genuine safety structure.\n\\end{itemize}\n\n\\noindent These findings provide initial characterization on one benchmark for hidden-state safety monitoring and identify layer selection as a critical confound that future work must address.\\looseness=-1\n",

    "newString": "\\citet{zhou2024alignment} show safety-relevant information distributes across layers and argue it originates in pre-training rather than alignment; our temporal probes target a distinct phenomenon---commitment transitions during reasoning---that is absent from pre-training data. Meanwhile,",
    "originalFile": "\\section{Related Work}\\label{sec:related-work}\n\n\\paragraph{Safety in Reasoning Models.}\nReasoning models introduce a qualitatively new failure mode: the model's own chain of thought can transition from safety-aware deliberation to harmful generation without adversarial input modification~\\cite{wang2025lrmsafety}.\n\n\\textit{Attack characterization.} Self-jailbreak has been documented with step-level annotations~\\cite{harmthoughts}, with longer chains increasing circumvention probability~\\cite{selfjailbreak}. H-CoT~\\cite{hcot2025} shows reasoning chains can be externally hijacked. Unfaithful reasoning~\\cite{chen2025unfaithful,turpin2023language} further motivates hidden-state monitoring that bypasses text. Safety-aligned models maintain refusal intentions during thinking but experience a sharp drop at output generation~\\cite{yin2025refusal_cliff,hagendorff2026lrm_jailbreak,hu2026refusal_dynamics,ahrend2026safer_traces}.\n\n\\textit{Detection.} CoT monitoring can detect reward hacking but optimization pressure promotes obfuscation~\\cite{baker2025monitoring}. CoT monitorability is valuable but fragile~\\cite{korbak2025cot_monitorability}. GuardReasoner~\\cite{xiang2025guardreasoner} trains a dedicated reasoning-based guard model.\n\n\\textit{Mitigation.} SafeThink~\\cite{safethink} steers early reasoning toward safety, SafeRemind~\\cite{saferemind} inserts entropy-triggered reminders, and Chain-of-Guardrails~\\cite{mao2025selfjailbreak_reasoning} proposes guardrail insertion during reasoning. None of these conducts a systematic modality comparison for commitment detection.\n\n\\paragraph{Hidden-State Safety Detection.}\nInternal activations can detect safety violations in standard LLMs. \\citet{azaria2023internal} demonstrate that internal states encode whether a model's output is truthful, motivating representation-based monitoring. \\citet{zhou2024alignment} show safety-relevant information distributes across layers, while \\citet{qi2025shallow_alignment} find that alignment operates primarily over early output tokens. \\citet{arditi2024refusal} identify a single refusal direction, \\citet{turner2023activation} steer behavior via activation addition without optimization, and \\citet{zhao2025harmfulness} show harmfulness and refusal are encoded separately. \\citet{joad2026refusal_multi} reveal eleven category-specific refusal directions, \\citet{templeton2024scaling} extract interpretable features at scale, and \\citet{wollschlager2025geometry} formalize multi-dimensionality through concept cones, consistent with our finding that single-direction methods are insufficient. TrajGuard~\\cite{trajguard} uses Mahalanobis distance from safe trajectories; SafeSwitch~\\cite{safeswitch} trains hidden-state classifiers; SIREN~\\cite{siren} monitors internal representations during generation; SafeDream~\\cite{safedream2026} proposes proactive world models for detection; HiddenDetect~\\cite{jiang2025hiddendetect} extends hidden-state monitoring to vision-language models; SafeInt~\\cite{safeint2025} applies representation intervention for defense; RCS~\\cite{rcs2026} uses Mahalanobis-based contrastive scoring for jailbreak detection. These methods assume threats from external input manipulation and operate at the token level on instruction-tuned models. The self-jailbreak setting differs because transitions are internal and span multiple reasoning steps. \\citet{kadali2025internal} decompose internal layer representations to separate jailbreak from benign prompts; their results confirm that layer-wise structure carries safety-relevant signal. Concurrently, \\citet{chan2025probing} probe safety commitment in LLMs, complementing our focus on metric-induced layer selection bias. We reproduce TrajGuard (\\cref{sec:negative}) and find its geometric approach fails because discriminative signal resides in minor PCA components.\n\n\\paragraph{Probing Methodology and Layer Selection.}\nCausal abstraction~\\cite{geiger2025causal} formalizes when high-level causal models faithfully describe neural computation, grounding use of internal representations. Linear probing spans layer-wise classifiers~\\cite{alain2017understanding}, sentence probing~\\cite{conneau2018probing}, and surveys~\\cite{belinkov2019analysis,belinkov2022probing,rogers2020primer}. Control tasks~\\cite{hewitt2019control} distinguish structure from memorization (we adopt these; \\cref{app:control}). Information-theoretic and causal formulations address probe complexity~\\cite{voita2020information,pimentel2020information,ravfogel2020null,ravfogel2022rlace,belrose2023leace,elazar2021amnesic}. \\citet{marks2024geometry} find emergent linear structure in LLM truth representations, consistent with our safety commitment separability. That information distributes non-uniformly across layers is well established~\\cite{tenney2019bert,jawahar2019bert}. Our contribution is orthogonal: the known layer-wise information gradient interacts with metric choice to produce systematic selection bias in safety probes. Most directly, \\citet{boxo2025linear_probes} show linear probes can rely on textual evidence rather than deeper representations; our metric bias analysis provides a complementary mechanism: shallow-layer probes exploit compressed representations that inflate crossing rate, a geometric effect consistent with text-leakage at layers where representations have not yet diverged from surface features. \\citet{alvarez2026transport_geometry} apply hidden-state geometry to step-level hallucination detection concurrently with our work. Despite this foundation, layer selection remains underexamined: most studies select by a single metric without sensitivity analysis, a practice we show can reverse conclusions (\\cref{sec:layer-results}). \\citet{pais2026linear_probe_scaling} show that multi-layer ensembling recovers performance where single-layer probes fail, a complementary solution to the sensitivity problem we diagnose. Our approach is correlational; causal validation~\\citep{geiger2025causal,belrose2023leace,li2024iti} is needed to confirm probed directions are causally active.\n\n\\paragraph{Alternative Detection Approaches.}\nBOCPD~\\cite{adams2007bayesian} detects abrupt distributional shifts; CCS~\\cite{burns2023discovering} discovers latent truth directions via consistency constraints; RepE~\\cite{zou2023representation} identifies concept directions through mean differences. We adapt all three to temporal commitment detection (\\cref{sec:negative}). BOCPD and CUSUM fail because commitment is gradual. CCS provides no gain because its consistency objective misaligns with temporal structure. RepE shows inconsistent performance across models (0.756 on QwQ, 0.559 on R1-32B). These failures motivate supervised probes with principled layer selection as the minimal effective methodology.\n",

Automated behaviors (\"from now on when X\", \"each time X\", \"whenever X\", \"before/after X\") require hooks configured in settings.json - the harness executes these, not Claude, so memory/preferences cannot fulfill them. Also use for: permissions (\"allow X\", \"add permission\", \"move permission to\"), env vars (\"set X=Y\"), hook troubleshooting, or any changes to settings.json/settings.local.json files. Examples: \"allow npm commands\", \"add bq permission to global settings\", \"move permission to user settings\", \"set DEBUG=true\", \"when claude stops show X\". For simple settings like theme/model, suggest the /config command.\n- keybindings-help: Use when the user wants to customize keyboard shortcuts, rebind keys, add chord bindings, or modify ~/.claude/keybindings.json. Examples: \"rebind ctrl+s\", \"add a chord shortcut\", \"change the submit key\", \"customize keybindings\".\n- simplify: Review changed code for reuse, quality, and efficiency, then fix any issues found.\n- fewer-permission-prompts: Scan your transcripts for common read-only Bash and MCP tool calls, then add a prioritized allowlist to project .claude/settings.json to reduce permission prompts.\n- loop: Run a prompt or slash command on a recurring interval (e.g. /loop 5m /foo). Omit the interval to let the model self-pace. - When the user wants to set up a recurring task, poll for status, or run something repeatedly on an interval (e.g. \"check the deploy every 5 minutes\", \"keep running /babysit-prs\"). Also use when the user wants a one-time scheduled run (\"run this once at 3pm\", \"remind me to check X tomorrow\").\n- claude-api: Build, debug, and optimize Claude API / Anthropic SDK apps.
